Corporate actions: four lanes, no residue

A dated future can carry an awkward adjustment to its expiry and settle it there. A perpetual has no expiry to absorb it. So the design adjusts only where the arithmetic is exact and terminates the contract everywhere else.

"Because the contract has no expiration, nothing a stock does to it can be carried to an expiry and flushed there: it is adjusted only where the arithmetic is exact, and terminated where it is not"— Corporate Actions Operations Playbook v1.2, Sep 16, 2026

One classifier sends every event to exactly one lane: "declared cash → A; whole-number ratio → B; anything else with value or a re-basing → C if the name survives with a Consolidated NBBO, D if it does not". Rule 43.15 says "four categories, and into no others."

Lane Events Treatment Example
A · Cash Ordinary, special, capital-gain, return-of-capital, ADR gross On the ex-date, prior settlement steps down by the declared per-share amount (4 decimals) under OCC Rule 2803(o); the value arrives through variation $0.50 dividend: 32.40 → 31.90; a 10-lot long gains $500
B · Whole split 2:1, 3:1, 4:1, whole stock dividends Contracts ×N, prior settlement ÷N to $0.0001; same symbol, ID and 100-share unit 3-for-1: 10 × 100 × $300 = 30 × 100 × $100
C · Structural, name survives Reverse and odd splits, spin-offs, valued rights, stock-for-stock mergers Trades through the last cum session, settles at that DSP, terminates; a successor lists next session with zero open interest "No one valued SPN."
D · No continuing market Cash merger, delisting, liquidation, NBBO loss, halt over 10 trading days, OCC stops clearing Cash settlement at the first available of: deal cash → last reliable NBBO midpoint → primary official close → OCC value $52.00 cash merger

Four rules hold across the lanes.

  • Ex-date symmetry. The Index and the prior settlement step down together. The premium does not move, and funding charges nothing extra. The Brief calls this "symmetry, not a patch." Funding never pauses for a corporate action.
  • No conversion, baskets or valuation. "the market converts the ratio, not the Exchange. Nothing about the 100-share unit changed, and no one divided a contract by ten."
  • Resting orders are purged before every A or B event.

In-place adjustment of structural events only works when an expiry flushes the residue. Eurex's R-factor shows the drift otherwise, with one contract moving from 1,000.0000 to 998.5180 shares in February 2026. A reverse split also cannot be count-adjusted without creating fractional contracts.

The desk makes three moves on every event: decide, report to OCC, publish, using one of four pre-written Corporate Action Alerts. "No one estimates a value — every number is an issuer's declared amount or a market price."

A final settlement happens only by rule. The Termination Notice designates the last session as the contract's maturity date, following the September 2020 precedent when OneChicago accelerated its maturities. An OCC standing rule then sets the price. On its last day, a terminating name "is an expiring future".

§871(m), made clean

Foreign holders of a delta-one position on a U.S. dividend payer face dividend-equivalent withholding under §871(m). Lane A's published factor doubles as the withholding base: base = factor × 100 × contracts. A non-U.S. long receives its gain less 30% (or the treaty rate) on that base. "It does not avoid §871(m) — it makes §871(m) clean." This opens the product to foreign flow. How to Win cites the July 2026 Korea leverage unwind, which margin-called 1.2 million retail accounts, as evidence of that demand.

Rival venues handle dividends three other ways. CME forecasts them in the basis. Kalshi passes them through funding. Coinbase and Nadex do not adjust. Only Bitnomial adjusts in cash by rule, as AP does.

"Four lanes. No conversion, no valuation, ever."— MIAX Brief End-to-End, Sept 2026

Sources: Corporate Actions Operations Playbook v1.2 (Sep 16, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Unified Technical Specification v1.0, Vol. II (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); OCC Clearing Brief (Sept 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End (Sept 2026); How to Win (Sept 2026)

What OCC must decide, and what it builds

OCC has one decision to make, and everything waits on it. Funding must be variation: a novated Funding Adjustment that moves between clearing members on the existing daily wire, nets to zero and never touches the venue. Nothing goes to the CFTC or SEC until OCC agrees (link 12 of the launch sequence).

The Clearing Brief ("Built once for OneChicago. Ready for Ovation.") makes three asks:

  1. Onboard the product on Ovation, OCC's new clearing platform, restarting the dormant single-stock futures clearing. The Brief specifies "no ENCORE integration of any kind"; ENCORE is OCC's mainframe from 2000.
  2. Clear funding through members as variation.
  3. Run the corporate actions OCC already wrote: the NoDivRisk dividend engine (Rule 2803(o)) and the split count method. "Everything structural terminates clean: no open-interest conversion, ever."

MIAX Futures became OCC's fifth participant exchange when the clearing agreement was announced on May 20, 2026. As filed (SR-OCC-2025-008), that agreement covers futures "other than security futures", so it needs a written amendment. The amendment would cover funding as variation and the zero-sum control, and would extend the settlement-price indemnity to funding rows, offered up front on the model of OneChicago's Amendment No. 1.

The class then enters through Schedule C and a new-product proposal. It also needs an extended-hours designation under OCC's ETH Procedure, which was approved September 23, 2026 (Order 34-106469).

OCC builds three new pieces. Everything else is configuration.

  1. Intake of the nightly cycle set.
  2. Computation of each member's cycle funding, with the per-name zero-sum control.
  3. A Funding Adjustment line on DDS (Data Distribution Service): a separately typed amount beside futures mark-to-market, one per OCC account.

The work spans eight OCC teams and seven decision families, each with a default. It comes with 32 conformance scenarios (T01–T32) and 8 worked fixtures.

Two points are marked [confirm-OCC], because only OCC can decide them and either could change a filed term:

  • The no-maturity sentinel. Will every OCC surface accept 99991231 / 9999-12-31? The runbooks call this "The riskiest confirmation in the program." OCC's clearing-member CSV layouts "require a full YYYYMMDD series date".
  • Full-session clearing. Will OCC clear through the full 23×5 session? If not, the trading hours change before filing.

OCC files one rule change, with the SEC under Rule 19b-4 and with the CFTC under Part 40, in three parts:

  • a Terminal-Settlement standing rule;
  • an express routing of the class to Rule 2803;
  • the agreement amendment.

This filing "gates clearing go-live, not the listing." Default needs no new rule. Accrued, unsettled funding is unsettled variation and ranks that way in OCC's existing waterfall.

OCC's own timetable sets the pace. External scenario testing resumed June 29, 2026. External Parallel Testing runs September 21, 2026 to January 2027. Ovation go-live is projected for 1H 2027.

"One product clears cleanly; one clearer decides the field."— OCC Clearing Brief, Sept 2026
Launch is keyed to Ovation go-live, projected 1H 2027 ("for a 2027 launch"), while three rivals filed perpetual SFPs on September 18, 2026.

Sources: OCC Clearing Brief (Sept 2026); Unified Technical Specification v1.0, Annex B (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Runbooks v1.9, C1–C6 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); How to Win (Sept 2026)

Onyx: what changes on the platform

The platform change is small and pinned. Annex A is a delta against MIAX's own posted v1.3a interfaces (July 31, 2026), "so scoping this build is reading, not discovery." Every addition extends a structure that already exists. "Nothing in this annex is a question."

Message 18

The feeds gain one message, type 18 "Funding Rate". Types 18, 19 and 20 were verified free on both ToM and DoM. Message 18 carries:

  • Instrument ID, timestamp and lock time;
  • Rate Type (P predicted, S settled);
  • the signed per-hour rate, already ÷24;
  • Mark and Index;
  • Index status (live / no-coverage / SIP feed dead / halt);
  • Funding Status (normal / paused).

A P record goes out every 5 seconds and freezes into an S record at each :00. The message is outside the refresh service and travels in MACH's skip-safe envelope, which keeps it backward-compatible. "Front ends take the Index from message 18: the Exchange provides it, so no firm computes its own."

Saying "never expires"

The perpetual reuses the Simple Instrument record with new values:

  • Instrument Type 'P';
  • Underlying Asset Type 'S';
  • Unit of Measure 'SHR', quantity 100;
  • Product Group Code = ticker + P.

The hard part is a date field on a contract that has no date. The answer is one sentinel per surface:

Surface Sentinel Why
Onyx Contract Date (YYYYMM) 999912 Maximum month
OCC series date (MMY) 99991231 8-digit series date
OCC and text layers (MatDt) 9999-12-31 ISO maximum
Onyx two-byte binary dates 65535 = June 6, 2149 Never 0, which decodes to 1970-01-01 and "reads as expired"
Daily reports "PERP" Human-readable

The clearing identity line is FUT / FFSCSX / 99991231 / 9999-12-31 / XMFE. One caution matters for every firm's code: "there is NO FIX SecurityType for a security futures product". In FIX 5.0 SP2, "SFP" means Structured Finance Product, so the perpetual clears as FUT. The order and execution interfaces (FOI, FEI, FXD) get no logic change, and "No firm re-certifies."

A second production instance

Onyx runs one recycle (5:05–5:40 p.m.) and one Live Order Window (5:45 p.m.) for all Financials products. Both fall inside the perpetual's session. The perpetual therefore runs on its own second Onyx instance, which recycles in the tape's 8–9 p.m. break. The rejected alternative was to let the perpetual go dark in the early evening while the stock trades.

The session follows the SIP's 23×5 production from December 6, 2026, in four Market States: Pre-Opening (in the break), Extended 1 (9:00 p.m.–9:30 a.m.), Regular (9:30 a.m.–4:00 p.m.) and Extended 2 (4:00–8:00 p.m.). "The perpetual trades only when the Reference Security's primary market trades." On the Nov 27 and Dec 24, 2026 half-days it closes at the tape's 1:00 p.m. Firms add one feed channel and session set. If the SIP moves its hours, the change is "one setting … one filing … one announcement … the arithmetic does not change."

Build order

Annex A fixes ten steps. Pin the release, then build the instrument record ("everything keys off it"), engine configuration, samplers and the lock pipeline, "freeze → ÷24 → stamp → status → always-a-row". Message 18, the funding file and the OCC nightly file follow in parallel, then the second instance, then Firm Test Bed and UAT. Only two items are true engine builds: a sampler-restart checkpoint and the second instance. MIAX types six values itself, each with a default.

The change set ships as the next release after v1.3a. MIAX's Technical Alert of September 28, 2026 sets Financials production for Sunday, November 8, 2026. The room's wording can be read as dating either v1.3a or the release after it. Annex E then sweeps every MIAX Futures web page. It finds 21 surfaces that change and says explicitly that every other page does not, "so this sweep is auditable".

65535"never expires" in two bytesJune 6, 2149, the maximum two-byte day count. Zero would decode to 1970 and read as expired.

Sources: Annex A Onyx Change Set (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Sentinel & Symbology Card v1.0 (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Annex E Site & Publications Change Catalog v1.2 (Jul 8, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Runbooks v1.9, X1–X9 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

The failures designed out

The controls assume that no one will notice a problem in time. Each failure therefore has a check that runs on its own, a recovery that runs on its own, and a log that a person reviews.

"Nothing in this chapter depends on a person noticing something in time. Each failure below has a check that runs on its own, and a recovery that runs on its own; the desk reviews the log."— Runbooks v1.9, O6, Sep 17, 2026

The operations desk's list (Runbook O6) names nine failures:

Failure Automatic check Automatic recovery
Corporate action missed Two independent sources, matched daily against every name Either source opens a record and pages the desk; nothing publishes until sources agree
Wrong number reaches OCC or a notice Numbers come from declared or market sources, "never typed"; "the Alert is generated from the record, not written" Hold and page with both values
Ex-date value missing next day Definitions run checks every open event Run stops, pages, reloads
Nightly file late or short a row Platform builds and sends it at the closing lock; row count checked against the cycle Auto-resend; page after the second failure
Zero-sum check fails OCC checks per name, per cycle That name waits; every other name settles
Onyx restarts mid-hour Checkpointed running average Resume; "the rate is never defaulted to 0 while valid samples exist"
OCC unavailable Funding locks at the Exchange regardless Rows kept; resend later
Member default Funding is variation Ranks as unsettled variation at OCC
Event with no notice Contract already paused Lane D waterfall "needs no judgment"

The platform's list (X9) adds four checks:

  • a lock always writes a row, and the row can never be changed;
  • SIP sequence numbers and heartbeats;
  • duplicate guarding on trade reports;
  • an empty-book check after every order purge.

The OCC list (C6) covers OCC downtime ("nothing is recomputed"), wrong positions, a failed zero-sum and OCC layout changes.

Finality is itself a control. "A funding lock is never corrected: it is final when published" (Rule 43.10(f)). A completed settlement is never reopened. An error in a DSP is reversed by the next cycle's mark. Error-trade review uses a ±5% no-bust range around the Mark, and it "never reopens a locked Funding Rate or a computed Daily Settlement Price."

Sources: Runbooks v1.9, O6, X9, C6, O4 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Annex E /market-regulation-policies (Jul 8, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

Autonomous by design: what runs itself, what a person signs

The runbooks break operations into jobs. Each job takes a record in, puts a record out and checks one against the other. Every number in the product is a declared amount or a market price, so no step needs judgment except the ones the law reserves to a person.

"Every step in Part 3 is a job with an input, an output and a check: the input a record, the output a record, the check a comparison of the output to its source before anything leaves the building."— Runbooks v1.9, Part 5, Sep 17, 2026

These run on their own: listing tests and limit lines; Schedule entries and day-before filings from templates; instrument records; locks, settlement prices and every file; corporate-action intake through to the Alert; surveillance thresholds; billing and the royalty statement; and the calendar, generated from T.

A person always signs these fifteen acts:

  1. a filing with the CFTC or the SEC;
  2. adoption of a Schedule entry and its limit line;
  3. a report, a facts submission or an instruction to OCC;
  4. an Alert or circular;
  5. the last day, the successor and any closing-only period;
  6. a fee or product-setting change;
  7. a payment;
  8. an exemption;
  9. an accountability order;
  10. an error-trade decision;
  11. a disciplinary decision;
  12. a market-maker designation;
  13. a firm's readiness sign-off;
  14. the go call and a manual halt;
  15. a Board approval.

The corporate-actions runbook adds one more signature inside the flow. For each dividend, "A person signs the row before it enters the file."

Three records and an agent

The program keeps three records, however they are stored:

  • the name register, one row per name for its life;
  • the event record, one row per corporate action;
  • the decision register, which holds every open parameter, set once with a default.

The decision register's rule is "Set it once, write it into the runbook it belongs to, and stop asking." Completion has the same test everywhere: "A step is done when its record exists." The room ships an _AI_READABLE folder of machine-readable copies so that an agent can do the work.

"An AI agent loaded with the machine-readable folder runs Part 3 as written — it prepares each step's record and stops where the step names a person"— Runbooks v1.9, Part 5, Sep 17, 2026

The tooling stays open: "which agent, sheet, queue or dashboard is the Exchange's choice and its experts' craft."

AP has no role in operations. The glossary entry for AP ends: "It runs no step here." Until first listing, and never beyond the 18-month longstop, the founder gives walkthroughs and written clarifications only, "not engineering, development, testing, operation, filing".

15acts a person always signsEverything else is a job with a record in, a record out and a check, which an AI agent can run as written.

Sources: Runbooks v1.9, Parts 4–5 and Appendix B (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); How to Win, Part 06 (Sept 2026); The Deal (Sep 30, 2026)

Liquidity: a workstream, not a hope

How to Win gives liquidity an owner, a cohort, an incentive and a test.

"Liquidity is a workstream with an owner, a cohort, an incentive and a test — not a hope."— How to Win, Sept 2026

The plan has four parts:

  • Owner. The Exchange's market-maker desk, under Rule 2.1.9.
  • Cohort. MIAX's own member market makers first, then the offshore perpetual trading firms. To join, a firm needs one feed channel, a conformance run and a designation at listing.
  • Test. These are the recommended terms; MIAX sets the final ones. They follow the B500 program certified June 16, 2026:
  • designation per name;
  • in the stock's regular hours, two-sided at least 90% of the time, at least 5 contracts a side, within the greater of $0.20 or 1.5× the stock's spread;
  • in the rest of the session, 50% of the time, 1 contract;
  • measured monthly, halts excluded;
  • two months short in a name loses the designation there;
  • designations carry automatically to successor listings.
  • Incentive. A market-maker fee of $0.40 per side, about 27% of the regular fee, with no rebates. Width, size and time are published (the Rule 3.3.2(e) holding-out standard). Incentives may stay confidential.

Four flows are expected to arrive without being paid for:

  • retail, through a Robinhood securities account holding the perpetual beside stock and options;
  • the carry desk: long stock, short perpetual, earning funding ("the hourly funding print is a borrow market");
  • the options desk, using a delta-one hedge that never rolls;
  • portfolio-margin offsets.

There is no liquidation engine: no auction, no automatic deleveraging, no insurance fund. A losing account meets its carrying firm's margin call. A failing carrying firm meets OCC's waterfall. "the offshore venues' risk engine is not in the path."

Sources: How to Win, Part 03A (Sept 2026); Runbooks v1.9, S3 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End, §08A (Sept 2026)

Ten chains, sixty-six links

The launch plan runs as ten parallel chains. The 66 links are numbered across all chains in the order they happen. The documents call it "the order, not a calendar". Every document is already drafted, so "No phase waits for drafting".

Three gates hold the order:

  • Link 12. Nothing goes to the Commissions until OCC agrees that funding is variation.
  • Link 31. Nothing is made public until the listing request files.
  • Links 41 and 50. Nothing lists until the CFTC order and a go check where every line reads yes.
Chain Starts Links Finish line
1 · The Deal Now 1 → 2 → 3 → 57 First listing recorded; royalty starts
2 · Clearing (OCC) Day 1 5 → 12 → 13 → 14 → 15 → 21 → 30 → 32 → 36 → 48 OCC rules in effect; series in Ovation
3 · Platform (Onyx) Day 1 6 → 16 → 23 → 24 → 47 Release live with 23-hour session
4 · Market data & Index Day 1 11 → 22 → 37 Vendors told
5 · Market makers Day 1 7 → 19 → 20 → 38 → 44 → 59 → 60 Quoting measured
6 · Firms Day 1 8 → 17 → 18 → 35 → 49 → 58 Customer sales open
7 · Regulatory set Day 1 9 → 10 → 27 → 28 → 29 → 31 → 39 → 40 → 41 → 42 → 45 Market Regulation items
8 · Public face Test bed 26 → 33 → 46 Site walked
9 · Messaging Day 1, quiet 4 → 25 → 34 → 43 → 55 Launch announced
10 · The Go All lines yes 50 → 51 → 52 → 53 → 54 → 56 T+1 payment ties out
After launch T 61 → 62 → 63 → 64 → 65 → 66 "Finished when never"

The links that carry the most weight:

  • 2 · Signing (Day 1). Four papers in one sitting; the signing payment wires and the OCC conversation opens.
  • 27–28 · Letter and meeting. The recognition letter goes to both Chairmen, followed by a joint staff meeting. The risk-disclosure brief goes to FINRA and NFA at that meeting.
  • 29 · Tests and approvals. The six listing tests are re-run for all 101 names. The Regulatory Oversight Committee approves No. 101 (MIAX), and the Board approves.
  • 31 · Filed together. The §41.23(b) request, Form 19b-7 and Form 19b-4 go in on the same day. The quiet period ends.
  • 41 · The order. The CFTC order and Form 19b-7 take effect. Form 19b-4 does not gate day one.
  • 50 · The go check. Every line must read yes, from the order and OCC's rules to a money chain proved end to end.
  • 52–54 · Listing day. The 101 list as one batch. A one-page ISG notice goes out at T−1. Names 1–101 trade at T.
  • 56 · T+1. The first cycle ties out at every firm. Customer postings plus rounding must equal the DDS Funding Adjustment line, sign reversed.
  • 57 · First listing recorded. The royalty starts. If this falls within 18 months of signing, the exclusivity longstop is met.

The after-launch coda is the running business: a new name (61), the twice-yearly limit review (62), corporate actions (63), the trading day (64), the quarterly royalty (65) and a new perpetual class on the same rails at the same rate (66). It closes with "Finished when never — this is the business."

Messaging rules: the Exchange controls the brand; the product is "new", never "first" or "only"; funding is never annualized in a headline.

66 linksten chains, three hard gatesLink 12 (OCC agrees), link 31 (filings go in together), links 41 and 50 (the order and the go check).

Sources: How to Win, Part 04 (Sept 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End, §10 (Sept 2026); Runbooks v1.9, Part 2 and R2 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

Why the design rejects crypto-perp features

The perpetual is the structure that won crypto. It carries more than 90% of global crypto-futures volume, over $200 billion a day in 2025, almost all of it offshore. AP keeps the structure. It drops every crypto feature that exists only because those venues have no clearinghouse.

"The structure that won crypto, brought to regulated equity."— OCC Clearing Brief, Sept 2026

The specification lists three things the design "deliberately doesn't carry":

  1. No funding cap and no interest-rate term. In the specification's words: "Crypto funding is a premium plus an interest component, clamped; ours is the pure premium passthrough, uncapped." The basis of a cash-settled equity future already includes carry, and containment happens upstream, through the collar, the median Mark, the hourly average and surveillance. Rule 43.10(f): "The Funding Rate is an uncapped pass-through; containment is upstream."
  2. No insurance fund and no auto-deleveraging (ADL). "Crypto venues need both because they have no clearinghouse." This product has OCC: gross customer margin, a guaranty fund and a default waterfall (OCC Rule 1006; Part 39). "No venue insurance fund exists and none is needed."
  3. Tape-following, not 24/7. The Index is the SIP feed, so funding runs only while the consolidated tape runs: "funding never runs on a synthetic index."
"Losses are mutualized by a CCP — never socialized by haircutting winners."— Unified Technical Specification v1.0, Jul 7, 2026 (rev. Sep 30, 2026)

On three points the design matches or beats the crypto pattern:

  • funding every hour, against crypto's typical eight-hour interval;
  • settled rows plus a predicted stream, in "Bitnomial's exact surface shape";
  • a published Mark and Index, with a free public history of the same values the clearinghouse settles.

One crypto practice survives, moved down to the broker. A carrying firm's duties include "auto-liquidation against OCC-published margin parameters". The venue has no liquidation engine, but a firm still closes out an account that misses a call.

Uncapped funding is a deliberate break from crypto; the risk is spiky rates in thin names, answered only by the market-maker program, collar, median and TWAP.

Sources: Unified Technical Specification v1.0, Vol. I and Vol. II §5.4 (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); OCC Clearing Brief (Sept 2026); Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8, Rule 43.10(f) (Sep 30, 2026)

The field, briefly

By late September 2026, four rivals had built or filed single-stock contracts, and Nadex had filed perpetuals on ten names. AP argues that the category is won on "listings, liquidity and limits", and that only an OCC-cleared venue can pull all three levers. "Fees are a schedule the Exchange sets and can change on a filing; a matching engine is a purchase. Neither is a moat."

Axis American Perpetual CME dated SSF KalshiEX Coinbase Derivatives Bitnomial
Structure No expiry; hourly funding Quarterly expiries; rolls No fixed maturity; daily funding No fixed expiry; hourly funding No expiry; funding 3×/day
Names Any eligible; 101 at launch 50+ mega-caps 58 AAPL as model 10
Dividends Ex-date factor on variation In the basis Via funding No adjustment Cash adjustment
Margin 15% (20% FINRA securities) 15% SPAN floor 15.50% ≥15% 15.25%
Limits 25,000 or accountability 200,000 50,000; 200,000 cap 200,000 200,000
Clearing OCC, >100 members CME Clearing Kalshi Klear Nodal Clear Bitnomial Clearinghouse

Fees per side: AP $1.50 ($0.40 for market makers); CME $0.60 plus quarterly rolls; Kalshi's perpetual schedule a 12.0 bps taker rate at the first tier, which is $30 on a $25,000 contract.

AP's moat is clearing. "The moat is the clearing, because clearing is where the capital is." Firms carry the perpetual in their existing FCM accounts and portfolio-margin it beside the stock and its options. "Every product a silo adds deepens the silo; every product this venue adds deepens the network." Chapter V covers each rival's legal footing.

Sources: How to Win, Parts 01–03 (Sept 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End, §03 (Sept 2026)

Design principles reusable for crypto classes

Once the machine runs, it does not care what the underlying is. OCC carries hourly funding inside variation, and firms have wired the four booking rules. From then on, any underlying with a reliable reference runs unchanged. A new class needs only a new reference feed and a contract multiplier. "The member-side build is one-time and the same for every future perpetual product (a new class needs at most one more product-list entry)."

The expansion order:

  1. U.S. equities, deeper: more names, ADRs, wrapper ETFs, an IPO gate.
  2. Crypto majors, BTC · ETH · SOL · XRP, about 91% of listable spot flow. This needs a registered per-second index licence; the first name files for §40.3 approval and the rest self-certify under §40.2.
  3. FX majors, plus gold and silver as currency pairs.
  4. Foreign indices, Nikkei 225 first, when licensed. "never the first".

Rates, marquee U.S. indices, copper, compute, pre-IPO names, credit and direct volatility stay out.

The legal route for crypto is simpler than for equities: "a class that is not a security future is CFTC-only, with no SEC filing and no security-futures customer rules." There are precedents:

  • OCC cleared Cboe Futures Exchange bitcoin futures from December 2017 to 2019.
  • The CFTC approved Kalshi's bitcoin perpetual, on CF Benchmarks' BRTI, on May 29, 2026.
  • Kalshi's gold and silver perpetuals went live September 10, 2026.

Each later class is the Exchange's own build. It falls under the same agreement at the same rate (Deal ¶3(d)), and AP delivers nothing further. Chapter VIII sets out the class program in full.

Thirteen principles that carry over

  1. Define the class once. Vary per name only identifiers and risk bounds, so listing becomes a schedule row.
  2. Extend existing structures only. A free message slot, a new enum value, a new CSV. No firm re-certifies.
  3. Say "never expires" on each surface with the field's maximum value, never zero.
  4. Use one Product per name, so controls scoped to a product hit one name.
  5. Funding is variation inside the clearinghouse's daily settlement. It is never a fee and never the venue's money.
  6. A lock is final, there is always a row, and each name's cycle sums to zero before money moves.
  7. Publish the funding history free, "fetched, not sent", so anyone can recompute it.
  8. Adjust only where the arithmetic is exact; otherwise terminate and relist.
  9. Every number is a declared amount or a market price. That lets operations be automated, with people signing only what the law reserves.
  10. Design failures out with automatic checks and logs, not vigilance.
  11. Build to the cycle, never to a constant.
  12. Bring vendors in first. "one vendor build of the message parser and the booking module reaches every firm on the platform".
  13. The oracle is the consolidated reference, with validity gates and an automatic pause on feed loss: "Exclusion, not estimation, is the reliability guarantee." For crypto this means a registered per-second index, a two-sided-and-not-crossed validity rule, and a pause instead of a synthetic price.
~91%share of listable crypto spot flow in BTC · ETH · SOL · XRPThe second class in the expansion order, on the same OCC rails and the same agreement.

Sources: How to Win, Part 04A (Sept 2026); Runbooks v1.9, F5, S1, S2 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Annex A Onyx Change Set (Jul 7, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Harmonization Submission (draft, Sept 2026)

Open risks in the machine

The design itself is finished. What remains open are confirmations and clocks held by other parties, and the room names each of them.

  • Sentinel acceptance. If any OCC surface rejects 9999-12-31 / 99991231, the instrument has no identity there. Ovation "has no perpetual or no-expiry concept".
  • Full-session clearing. If OCC will not clear the full 23×5 session, the trading hours change before filing.
  • Clocks. Ovation go-live is projected for 1H 2027. The SIP's 23×5 production starts December 6, 2026. No document says what happens if listing comes before that date.
  • The standing rule. Reading 17 CFR 41.25(c)(3) as authority for a standing class rule is called "the efficient reading" of text "drafted as a case determination". OCC counsel may resist it.
  • IDs on splits. Ovation may create a "new derivative" for every corporate action. That would break the ID continuity lanes A and B depend on.
  • Lock timing. A position held at a lock pays the whole hour, and one opened and closed between locks pays nothing. That behaviour is not on the surveillance list.
  • Tax on funding. The character, timing and Form 1099-B treatment of the hourly Funding Adjustment are left to each firm's tax team.
  • Street cost. The second instance means one more feed channel and session set at every firm.
The design is called "finished", yet two OCC confirmations could still change filed terms, and neither can be asked until after signing.

Sources: Runbooks v1.9, C1 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); OCC Clearing Brief (Sept 2026); Corporate Actions Operations Playbook v1.2, App. D (Sep 16, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); Risk Disclosure Supplement Brief (Sept 2026)

X → ∞ · Chapter IV

Chapter VThe legal road — recognition, not relief

American Perpetual's legal strategy asks for no new law. A single-stock perpetual is a contract for future delivery of one security, so by statute it is a "security future". The statute also excludes every security futures product from the definition of "swap". The path to listing is therefore the ordinary security-futures path, run in a fixed order:

  1. OCC agrees.
  2. A recognition letter goes to both Commission Chairmen.
  3. Three filings go in on the same day.

Every filing in the data room is fully drafted. As of the September 30, 2026 compilation, none had been filed.

"MIAX Futures therefore seeks recognition, not relief"— Harmonization Submission (draft), Sept 2026

MIAX Futures is the sole petitioner of record. AP drafted the package but "prepares and files no rule, submission or approval and presents nothing."

The posture: no exemption, no no-action, no pilot

The premise is that the product complies with the rules as written. The Harmonization Submission lists four things it does not request:

  • No exemptive relief. Cash settlement, 15% margin, position limits, surveillance, Section 31 and tax are "each the existing rule applied". Terminal settlement is compliance under 17 CFR 41.25(c)(2)(i) and (c)(3), "not an exemption from anything."
  • No no-action position. "MIAX Futures seeks recognition that the product is what it is, not an assurance that the staff will decline to recommend enforcement."
  • No new product category and no rulemaking precondition.
  • No pilot or innovation exemption.

What it does expect are the ordinary filings any new contract needs:

  • MIAX's Part 41 certifications;
  • Form 19b-7;
  • a Form 19b-4 for its margin rule;
  • OCC's clearing-rule change;
  • the FINRA/NFA disclosure supplement.

"None is an exemption from the security-futures framework; each is that framework, applied."

"The product does not need a new lane built for it, and it does not need relief to occupy the one it is in; it needs recognition that it already occupies an existing lane."— Harmonization Submission (draft), Sept 2026

This posture is the main contrast with CME. CME relaunched dated single-stock futures on conditional, revocable relief, with conditions: mega-caps only, contracts of 9 months or less, daily position reporting, a mandated market-impact study, and that exchange alone.

Sources: Harmonization Submission, Parts VIII–IX (draft, Sept 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End, §§03, 08 (Sept 2026); Runbooks v1.9, R1 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

The legal theory: futurity first, then the exclusion

The argument has two steps, and their order matters. Futurity puts the instrument in the security-future category. The statutory exclusion then keeps it clear of every swap question.

Step 1: it is a security future. CEA §1a(44) and Exchange Act §3(a)(55)(A) define a "security future" as "a contract of sale for future delivery of a single security or of a narrow-based security index". A security futures product (SFP) is a security future or an option on one (§1a(45)). The CFMA of 2000 put the category under joint SEC/CFTC jurisdiction. The Form 19b-7 and the rulebook also cite CEA §1a(27) as the source of "future delivery". The documents cite different paragraphs, but the point is the same.

Step 2: therefore it is not a swap. CEA §1a(47)(B)(i) expressly excludes any "security futures product" from "swap". The exclusion carries into "security-based swap" too. Exchange Act §3(a)(68)(A)(i) adopts the swap definition "without regard to paragraph (47)(B)(x)", which switches off only clause (x) and leaves (B)(i) in force. An SFP is therefore neither a swap nor a security-based swap. The swap-versus-future dispute being litigated over commodity perpetuals "does not reach a security futures product."

"Future delivery" is convergence, not a calendar

Futurity is the only contested element. The Submission reads it functionally. A cash-settled future delivers nothing, so "future delivery" must mean a price tethered to, and made to converge on, an underlying reference. An expiry date is one way to get convergence. Funding is another.

"The perpetual future achieves the same convergence continuously rather than terminally."— Harmonization Submission (draft), Sept 2026

Each hour, the funding rate equals the average of (mark − index)/index divided by 24. When the contract trades rich, longs pay; when it trades cheap, shorts pay. This is "precisely the arbitrage pressure that collapses basis at a dated expiry, applied every hour instead of once." The Submission claims the perpetual shows convergence "more cleanly than a dated future".

The cases cited:

  • The Kalshi Order (CFTC, May 29, 2026), treated as persuasive.
  • CME v. SEC (7th Cir. 1989), CFTC v. Erskine (6th Cir. 2008) and CFTC v. Standard Forex (E.D.N.Y. 1996). These locate a future in standardized, exchange-traded, centrally cleared price-risk transfer with convergence to a reference, not in physical delivery.
  • CFTC v. Zelener (7th Cir. 2004), distinguished as rolling spot FX with no clearinghouse and no convergence.

The observability bridge

The CFTC's perpetuals policy statement requires a reference "reliable at every funding interval, without interruption, for as long as the contract remains active." The Submission answers that a listed U.S. equity, referenced to the SIP consolidated NBBO, is "the paradigm of a deep, active, continuous, and reliable reference". The binding price, the 4:00 p.m. settlement, forms in the deepest market of the day. Reliability comes from exclusion, not estimation:

  • a sample counts only if the quote is two-sided and not crossed;
  • loss of the reference pauses funding;
  • permanent loss triggers a Terminal Settlement Event.
"Exclusion, not estimation, is the reliability guarantee."— Harmonization Submission (draft), Sept 2026

Funding is variation, not a bilateral swap payment stream. The Exchange cannot compute per-member cash, because after novation only OCC sees members' netted positions: "An 'exchange-computes-the-cash' design is therefore structurally foreclosed, not merely disfavored."

CME v. Selig (D.D.C., filed June 18, 2026) asks a court to treat funding-rate perpetuals as swaps; the Submission argues the SFP exclusion keeps this product outside that fight, and the suit is undecided (the CFTC moved to dismiss).

Sources: Harmonization Submission, Parts I–IV, VI (draft, Sept 2026); Form 19b-7, futurity paragraph (draft, 2026); Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8, Ch. 1 (Sep 30, 2026); How to Win, Part 02 (Sept 2026)

The Kalshi order: persuasive, not controlling

AP's theory leans on a precedent set four months before the data room closed. On May 28, 2026, KalshiEX LLC submitted a bitcoin perpetual (BTCPERP) under CFTC Regulation 40.3. On May 29, 2026, the CFTC approved it as a futures contract under CEA §5c(c)(4) (Press Release 9240-26). It issued a policy statement on perpetual contracts at the same time. The contract references CF Benchmarks' Bitcoin Real Time Index.

The Submission takes three things from the order:

  1. The holding. Funding "incentivizes the futures contract's price to converge to the spot market price", and "[t]hat terminal convergence is why a futures contract is a futures contract."
  2. The limit. The order covers only perpetuals on "bitcoin or other digital commodities … that have deep, active, and continuous spot market trading."
  3. The invitation. Perpetuals on other asset classes are directed to Regulation 40.3 review, case by case.

MIAX cites the order as persuasive reasoning on "future delivery", not as controlling authority. Its scope is limited to digital commodities, and it is in litigation. The equity case, the Submission argues, is cleaner, because the swap exclusion removes "the very classification dispute being litigated for commodity perpetuals."

"The single-name equity perpetual is the natural next case, and the cleanest one."— Harmonization Submission (draft), Sept 2026

The order moved markets. The Deal says the first crypto-perpetual approval took 9% off Cboe in a session and about 4% off CME and ICE, and it dates that reaction to June 2, 2026. How to Win dates the approval to May 29. The room does not reconcile the two dates; the reaction may simply have come in later sessions.

Kalshi kept moving after the order. Its gold and silver perpetuals went live around the clock on September 10, 2026. On September 18 it filed perpetual SFPs on 58 names.

May 29, 2026CFTC approves the first U.S. perpetual (Kalshi BTCPERP)The futurity reasoning AP borrows for equities; persuasive, not controlling.

Sources: Harmonization Submission, Parts I, IV and fn. 5–6, 12 (draft, Sept 2026); How to Win, Part 04A (Sept 2026); The Deal, p2 (Sep 30, 2026)

The Harmonization letter

The first regulatory act is a 17-page confidential letter. It goes from MIAX Futures to SEC Chairman Paul S. Atkins and CFTC Chairman Michael S. Selig, and asks for recognition that the perpetual satisfies the existing security-future definition. It goes out only after OCC agrees that funding is variation (link 27 of the launch sequence). A joint staff meeting follows (link 28).

  • Channel. The 2026 SEC–CFTC Harmonization MOU, signed March 11, 2026. The letter relies on Article III(2)(a) ("Clarifying product definitions through joint interpretations and rulemakings"), III(3) and III(5)(c). Article III(5)(c) reaffirms the SFP oversight MOU of March 17, 2004.
  • Form. Direct correspondence, not the initiative's public written-input channel. MIAX Futures signs as "the one requesting party".
  • Confidentiality. Requested under 17 CFR 200.83 and 145.9 and FOIA Exemption 4, for 10 years, renewable.

The letter places the product against a live record:

  • CME v. Selig, No. 1:26-cv-02157 (D.D.C., filed June 18, 2026).
  • The Commissions' Joint Request for Comment on the definitions of "swap" and "security-based swap". It was issued June 18 and published June 24, 2026 (91 FR 37873). Comments closed August 24, 2026. Its topics include "the scope of certain exclusions from the swap definition" and "novel or emerging products".

The letter is not an approval. "The recognition is an input to the ordinary filing; nothing issues from this letter itself." Its durable form is the CFTC's own order in the §41.23(b) proceeding.

Part IX of the letter lays out four workstreams to a live product:

  1. Pre-filing coordination, which is the letter itself.
  2. Listing authorization: Form 19b-7, Part 41 listing, a Form 19b-4 margin rule "as OneChicago's did", and one §41.23(b) request for the 101 names.
  3. The FINRA/NFA risk-disclosure supplement, which gates customer sales.
  4. The OCC clearing workstream, which gates clearing go-live, not listing.
The confidential letter seeks 10-year treatment for "the funding methodology", but the public §41.23(b) request and Form 19b-7 disclose the same mechanics in full.

Sources: Harmonization Submission, Parts I, VIII–XI and App. B (draft, Sept 2026); Runbooks v1.9, R1 steps 4–5 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

One §41.23(b) request for 101 names

Most security futures list by self-certification. MIAX instead asks the CFTC for voluntary approval of all 101 inaugural names in one submission. The aim is that "the framework and the first names stand on an affirmative CFTC action." Every later name then certifies forward.

The 23-page draft, "MIAX Futures DCM Submission No. 26-[ ]", is addressed to Christopher Kirkpatrick, Secretary of the CFTC, through the CFTC Portal. It is a Request for Voluntary Commission Approval under CEA §5c(c)(4), Regulation §41.23(b) and §40.5. It carries the §41.22 certifications for each name, §41.25 compliance, and a core-principle certification. Core Principle 7 covers the public historical-funding endpoint. Core Principle 16 covers conflicts, because name No. 101 is MIAX's own parent. Appendix A is the Specifications Supplement with the Schedule of 101 names. Appendix B is the Index Source Appendix.

The clock. The Commission acts within 45 days of a complete submission under §40.5. It can extend that for novel products, and MIAX "does not object". Trading starts only on the order. The filing is public, with no §40.8 request, and is posted on the Exchange's website the same day.

After the order. Each later name lists by §41.23(a) certification, received by the business day before it trades, as one Schedule row. Framework changes go under §41.24. The Submission calls this "the ordinary 'reviewed once, certify forward' security-futures mechanism, applied with an affirmative order at the front of the roster."

Eligibility: the six conditions

Rule 43.2(a) restates the security-futures standards as six conjunctive conditions, all judged from public information:

  1. An eligible class under SEC Rel. 34-61027 (Nov 19, 2009), which means any options-eligible security; plus Section 12 registration and reporting compliance, with a 30-day cure.
  2. An NMS stock listed on a national securities exchange.
  3. At least 7,000,000 publicly held shares, excluding Section 16(a) holders.
  4. At least 2,000 holders.
  5. At least 27,500,000 shares of volume over 12 months.
  6. A lowest close of at least $5.00 over 5 business days.

Against Cboe's options listing guideline, the volume test is "more restrictive, by more than eleven times": 27.5 million shares against 2.4 million. A name that stops qualifying goes closing-only by Corporate Action Alert and may be withdrawn.

No. 101 is an Affiliate Security. MIAX listing its own parent's stock requires:

  • a Market Regulation determination;
  • Regulatory Oversight Committee approval before listing, from a committee of at least three Public Directors;
  • quarterly reports;
  • an annual independent accounting-firm review;
  • written notice to both Commissions within 5 Business Days of any noncompliance.

The models are NYSE Rule 497, NYSE Texas Rule 24, Nasdaq Rule 4370 and Cboe BZX Rule 14.3(e).

The other §41.22 certifications rest on existing facts:

  • dual trading is satisfied by an electronic platform with no floor and no override;
  • manipulation is contained by the SIP midpoint, the median Mark, the collar and the hour-long average;
  • surveillance is ISG coverage of each name, a listing condition under Rule 2.1.18.
45 daysthe CFTC's §40.5 review clockExtendable for a novel product; MIAX "does not object". Trading starts only on the order.

Sources: Rule 41.23 Listing Submission (draft, Sept 2026); Form 19b-7, §6(h)(3) analysis (draft, 2026); Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8, Rules 43.2–43.3 (Sep 30, 2026); Specifications Supplement v1.3 (Sep 22, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

The SEC side: Form 19b-7, Form 19b-4 and Section 6(g)

The SEC side has two filings and one open question. Form 19b-7 takes effect on the CFTC's action. Form 19b-4 runs on its own clock, and the first day of trading does not wait for it.

Form 19b-7: the listing rules

The 16-page Form 19b-7 is filed once, through EFFS, under Exchange Act §19(b)(7). It adopts:

  • the Rule 43.2 listing standards;
  • the customer-protection rules (2.1.14 G on sales practices, 2.1.18 on the joint framework and ISG, 2.1.19 on risk-disclosure delivery);
  • Chapter 43 and its conforming amendments, including new Rule 6.21, the public funding endpoint.

It takes effect under §19(b)(7)(B) upon the CFTC's §41.23(b) approval. The SEC then has 60 days to summarily abrogate it. Later position-limit changes, including the conversion of Nos. 35, 39 and 77 at their first reviews, go to the CFTC under §41.24 only, with no new 19b-7.

The form also answers Exchange Act §6(h)(3) clause by clause, from (A) to (L), following OneChicago's 2003 analysis. Clause (E), linked clearing, remains deferred under §6(h)(7).

Form 19b-4: the customer-margin rule

The 11-page Form 19b-4 asks for SEC approval under §19(b)(2) of new Rule 3.3.2, Customer Margin for Security Futures Products. It has to be a separate filing. The federal regulations allow offsets, the portfolio-margin exclusion and the dealer exclusion only through SRO rules "effective in accordance with section 19(b)(2)".

The rule:

  • sets margin on OCC scan ranges, never below 15% of current market value, except for offsets and portfolio-margin accounts;
  • adopts the 2020 Strategy-Based Offset Table (85 FR 75112);
  • creates Security Futures Dealers, Market Participants who hold themselves out with continuous two-sided quotes under the Market Maker Program, within a maximum width and a minimum share of the session. This is the analog of OneChicago Rule 515(n). Their positions are margined on OCC clearing-level parameters rather than customer margin. They register on a new Chapter 70 form.

The models are OneChicago SR-OC-2002-01 (2002), CME SR-CME-2026-001 (approved July 13, 2026) and Coinbase Derivatives SR-COIN-2026-001. The SEC has 45 days from Federal Register publication, extendable to 90. MIAX does not consent to the extension. The product can list before the rule is approved. Until then the federal rules apply directly and nobody takes the dealer exclusion. Market makers register as Security Futures Dealers on the day of approval (link 59).

MIAX does not object to a CFTC §40.5 extension but does not consent to an SEC §19(b)(2) extension, so the two clocks are treated differently on purpose.

Section 6(g): is MIAX Futures already registered?

A DCM that trades security futures must be notice-registered as an exchange under Exchange Act §6(g). On December 11, 2020, Minneapolis Grain Exchange, now MIAX Futures, filed a Form 1-N (Rel. 34-90785, Dec 22, 2020). The SEC's exchange list, last updated January 7, 2026, shows only "Minneapolis Grain Exchange, Inc. (formerly registered)".

So the first of six asks to MIAX is one of two things: confirm that the 2020 notice still stands, or, by default, file a fresh Form 1-N, which "takes effect on filing". CME filed a fresh one on April 10, 2026 (Rel. 34-105336), and it was acknowledged 19 days later.

Sources: Form 19b-7 (draft, 2026); Form 19b-4, Customer Margin Rule 3.3.2 (draft, 2026); Runbooks v1.9, R1 and Appendix A (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026); MIAX Brief End-to-End, §08A (Sept 2026)

Rulebook Chapter 43

AP drafted the full rule text. The Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8 runs 35 pages. It adds one chapter and small conforming amendments, and confirms 12 existing rules unchanged. Chapter 43 takes the next slot after the financial-index Chapters 40–42, and uses the Bloomberg 500 chapter as its house-style skeleton. "nothing here is novel for novelty's sake."

Rule Subject Key text
43.2 Listing standards Six conditions; closing-only on failure; (d) Affiliate Security
43.3 / 43.22 Specifications Supplement Class terms once; one Schedule row per name
43.4–43.7 Unit, tick, hours, no expiry 100 shares, invariant; $1.00 tick; P&L = Mark change × unit ± net Funding Adjustments
43.8–43.9 Index and Mark Valid NBBO midpoint; median(last, bid, offer); absent input = pause

43.15–43.16 Corporate actions; Terminal Settlement "four categories, and into no others"; three classes of Terminal Settlement Event; cash waterfall

Outside Chapter 43, the package does five things:

  • adds defined terms (Perpetual Future, Funding Rate, Settlement Period, Terminal Settlement Event);
  • adds Rule 2.1.14 G and Rules 2.1.18, 2.1.19, 3.3.2, 5.12(E) and 6.21;
  • amends Rule 5.6 so the perpetual opens at 9:00 p.m. ET;
  • leaves MIAX's own clearing house (Chapter 21) out of it, since Rule 2100.00 already routes OCC-cleared contracts to OCC;
  • withdraws proposed Rule 2100.06.

Sources include CME, CFE, KalshiEX's BTCPERP, Coinbase's Perp Style Futures (the model for hourly computation) and OneChicago NoDivRisk. The package went from v2.1 (Aug 19) to v2.8 (Sep 30, 2026) in six weeks.

"the published number is the booked number"— Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8, Rule 43.10 drafting basis, Sep 30, 2026

Sources: Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8 (Sep 30, 2026); Form 19b-7, Exhibit 4 (draft, 2026)

Risk disclosure: one ask of FINRA

Customer sales wait on one document that neither MIAX nor AP writes. FINRA and NFA must supplement the uniform Security Futures Risk Disclosure Statement. The 4-page Risk Disclosure Supplement Brief hands the drafters every product fact, section by section, and makes one ask.

"The one document FINRA and NFA must write — and everything they need to write it."— Risk Disclosure Supplement Brief, Sept 2026

The Statement was approved by the SEC in 2002. Its current form is the 2020 Statement and Supplement (FINRA Regulatory Notice 20-28; implemented September 14, 2020). It describes only dated contracts. The nearest precedent is the 2010 supplement's dividend paragraph, written for OneChicago NoDivRisk: "this product's own ancestor."

Three product facts for retail:

  1. No expiration. A position lasts until it is offset or a Terminal Settlement Event occurs, and every ending is in cash.
  2. Funding. It is charged hourly, in either direction, with no cap. A locked rate is final. Funding nets to zero, settles daily, and "is not a fee." A position held at a lock bears the whole hour. A position opened and closed between locks bears none. A partly halted hour settles a full hour, computed from its live samples.
  3. Endings. A contract can end by corporate-action termination, by market-loss termination (including a halt of ten consecutive trading days), by a closing-only restriction, or by withdrawal.

The changes to the Statement are few. §2.6.2 on expiration becomes inapplicable. The margin section must "state both" levels, because the Statement still says 20%. "Everything else in the Statement holds for this product exactly as written."

The ask: FINRA Rule 4210, from 20% to 15%. The federal floor fell to 15% on December 24, 2020. FINRA's Rule 4210(c)(5) and (f)(10) still hold security futures at 20% in a securities account. A non-portfolio-margined securities customer therefore "posts a third more than a futures-account customer for the same position." The room asks FINRA to conform.

The sales-practice overlay is already drafted as Rule 2.1.14 G.4. A recommendation must disclose three things: no expiration, hourly accrual with daily settlement, and termination on a Terminal Settlement Event. A new account-approval form adds a checkbox for "no-expiration, funding-bearing security futures."

15% vs 20%futures account vs FINRA securities accountA non-portfolio-margined securities customer "posts a third more"; FINRA is asked to conform Rule 4210 to the federal floor.

Sources: Risk Disclosure Supplement Brief (Sept 2026); Form 19b-7, Rules 2.1.14 G and 2.1.19 (draft, 2026); Runbooks v1.9, R6 (Sep 17, 2026; rev. Sep 30, 2026)

Surveillance through ISG

Surveillance runs on credentials MIAX already holds. Its existing Intermarket Surveillance Group membership covers each Reference Security (Rule 2.1.18; §41.22(g); Exchange Act §6(h)(3)(I)). Nothing is asked of ISG as a body: "no filing, no approval, no fee, no new agreement."

The 13-page ISG Surveillance Brief maps five numbers that move money:

  • the Index;
  • the Mark;
  • the Funding Rate;
  • the Daily Settlement Price;
  • news, covered by Rule 43.23's ban on trading by issuer insiders and holders of material non-public information.

Each number is kept as a 5-second series. The manipulation map names "the funding push, marking the settlement window, moving the Index in thin hours". The thin hours are 9:00 p.m.–9:30 a.m. and 4:00–8:00 p.m. The Brief prices the push: at a $180.00 Mark, a 1-basis-point hourly rate requires a 0.24% average premium sustained for the hour, and moves $1.80 per contract.

Because every lock and every settlement is final, surveillance never asks for a price to be recomputed.

"the surveillance question in this class is never 're-run the number'; it is always 'who did it, and in what accounts'."— ISG Surveillance Brief, Sept 2026

Halts follow the stock automatically. "What the stock's listing market decides, the contract follows within the same five-second sample." Records join on Instrument ID, symbol, SIP ticker and a nanosecond clock. Three things are confirmed before listing:

  • the ISG membership tier and its single-equity data coverage;
  • coverage of each name before it is certified;
  • a named representative.

At T−1 a one-page ISG notice with 11 fields goes out (link 53).

The Brief's own push example "does not establish an attack-cost bound", and lock-timing behaviour (enter just after a lock, exit just before) is not on its vector list.

Sources: ISG Surveillance Brief (Sept 2026); Rule 41.23 Listing Submission, §41.22(g) (draft, Sept 2026)

OCC's own rule filing

OCC files one ordinary rule change, with the SEC under Rule 19b-4 and with the CFTC under Part 40. It has three parts:

  1. A Terminal-Settlement standing rule under 17 CFR 41.25(c)(3) and SEC Rule 6h-1(b)(3). It applies three statutory factors: fairness to buyers and sellers, a fair and orderly market, and consistent interpretation.
  2. An express provision that the class adjusts under Rule 2803. Rule 2804(a) otherwise governs cash-settled contracts "except as expressly provided otherwise".
  3. The clearing-agreement amendment. The May 20, 2026 agreement (SR-OCC-2025-008) reaches only futures "other than security futures".

The By-Laws, the default waterfall and the recovery-and-wind-down plan need no change. The filing "gates clearing go-live, not the listing." The room flags one weak point: using the final-settlement rule as authority for a standing class rule is called "the efficient reading", and OCC counsel may resist it.

The July 2026 orders letting CME close-settle its dated futures are cited as comfort only, and AP does not rely on them. They are the CFTC order of July 16, 2026 (91 FR 45640) and SEC Rel. 34-105882.

Sources: Rulebook Insertion & Amendment Package v2.8, Part III (Sep 30, 2026); OCC Clearing Brief (Sept 2026); Form 19b-7 (draft, 2026)

The Brief's reading is that the filings validate the category but not the venues. Since OneChicago closed, no new venue has completed the full equity-SFP stack. That stack needs three parts: notice registration on the securities side, ISG coverage, and a clearinghouse that carries security futures beside stock and options. Only OCC does the last. MIAX holds the DCM licence, the ISG seat and the OCC relationship.

Status: everything drafted, nothing filed

At the September 30, 2026 compilation, every paper was a final draft with blanks: "[Date of mailing]", "Submission No. 26-[ ]", "File SR-[ ]-2026-[ ]", Board approval "[ ], 2026", and the signatory "[Chief Regulatory Officer / General Counsel]". The order of steps is fixed, and none can begin before the deal is signed. The numbers are link numbers from the launch sequence.

  1. 12. OCC agrees that funding is variation.
  2. 9–10. The brackets in the filing set are filled. MIAX files a fresh Form 1-N or confirms the 2020 notice, and confirms ISG coverage name by name.
  3. 27–28. The letter goes to both Chairmen and the joint staff meeting is held. The disclosure brief goes to FINRA and NFA.
  4. 29. The six listing tests are re-run for all 101 names. The Regulatory Oversight Committee approves No. 101, and the Board approves.
  5. 31. The §41.23(b) request, Form 19b-7 and Form 19b-4 are filed on the same day, and the quiet period ends.
  6. 39–40. Staff questions are answered; the founder supplies the facts. FINRA and NFA file the supplement.
  7. 41. The CFTC order issues, and Form 19b-7 takes effect.
  8. 42 and 45. The fee schedule is filed on the ten-day rail. Market Regulation's launch items follow.
  9. 58–59. Customer sales open. Market makers register as Security Futures Dealers once Rule 3.3.2 is approved.
Form 19b-7 says the Exchange "has socialized the point with the staffs of the CFTC and the SEC through their joint harmonization process", but the letter that would do so is undated and unsent.
